✻ Churned for 55s

❯ Wait, what? I never want to wait 60s to finalize a command, don't get         
  confused                                                                      

● Stop Task
  ⎿  tail -f /tmp/claude-1002/-home-dev/dc451b74-1043-4c6b-906b-c181f031c4d
     9/tasks/b1cuhx1b4.output | grep -E --line-buffered
     "onResults|RESULT:|executeMacroCommand|… · stopped

● Right, sorry — to be clear: that 60s wait is what's currently in the code, not
  what I'm proposing. It's why nothing fired for your test command. The reason
  it's there: isUnambiguousVoiceMatch() in UMAssistedAccessibilityService.kt:609
  is hardcoded to always return false, with a comment saying early-stop was
  previously wired to a live predicate but caused a "session flap" bug — it was
  closing the session prematurely on the first partial fragment (e.g.
  "go"/"speed") before the user finished speaking, then immediately restarting.
  So it was disabled entirely rather than fixed, and the recognizer now always
  waits out the full 60s silence timeout to finalize.

  The fix is to make the predicate actually match against the known command
  vocabulary (macro phrases, facility names) instead of accepting any fragment —
  so "Resume career" would trigger early-stop once the partial transcript is a
  full recognized phrase, but a bare "speed" wouldn't. That's a real code change
  to voice-matching logic, which is the same sensitive area you previously told
  me not to touch unilaterally.

  Want me to implement that (wire isUnambiguousVoiceMatch to check partials
  against FacilityVocabulary/macro phrase matching), or would you rather shorten
  the 60s timeout as a simpler stopgap first?

● Early-stop fired, but look at the last line — onResults came back empty. The
  match stabilized (Resume career appeared twice within 6ms — SODA just re-emits
  the same partial in a tight batch, not because it settled over real time), so
  stopListening() fired almost instantly, before the recognizer had finished
  flushing a clean final hypothesis. So now it's fast but silently drops the
  command.

  The fix needs a real time-based settle delay, not just "two consecutive
  identical partials" (which can arrive milliseconds apart). Let me move the
  debounce into VoiceListener itself with a short delay before actually
  stopping.

● The settle-timing is correct, but stopListening() itself is the broken part on
  this on-device engine — it's discarding the buffered hypothesis instead of
  finalizing it. I'll redesign so we act directly on the confirmed partial

● Found the real gap: executeMacro is a two-line stub — no interpreter loop
  exists yet. MacroDefinition/MacroStep/MacroAction are fully designed and
  startCareer/finishCareer are written against real captured screens, but
  nothing walks the steps, matches OCR text, dispatches taps, or produces a

  is unimplemented.

  This means REQ-A19/A20/A21 — the three hard 1.0-alpha blockers — aren't
  actually done, contrary to what I told you earlier. The work needed is a real
  macro interpreter:

  - On each tick: capture screen text (via the existing OCR/corpus pipeline),
  re-check isInUma (REQ-SF7), find the first MacroStep whose matches predicate
  hits.
  - Dispatch the matched action: TapText (locate the text on screen, tap it,
  respecting AutoRunMacros.NEVER_TAP), TapWindowFraction (tap a window-relative
  point), Decision (consult stored defaults per MacroMode, or stop and return
  NEEDS_USER), Terminal (stop, COMPLETED).
  - Enforce maxSteps/maxDurationMs, and stop cleanly with UNRECOGNISED_SCREEN if
  nothing matches.

  This is a meaningfully sized implementation, not a quick patch. Want me to go
  ahead and build the interpreter now, or would you rather scope/review the
  approach first (e.g. how it should reuse the existing OCR/corpus-match code
  you already have for training-sweep/no-choice-advance)?

      1114 +    private fun findAndTapText(gen: Int, text: String, what: 
           +String): Boolean {                                           
      1115 +        if (AutoRunMacros.isForbiddenTapTarget(text)) {      
      1116 +            Log.w(TAG, "Refusing macro tap on forbidden targe
           +t: $text")                                                   
      1117 +            return false                                     
      1118 +        }                                                    
      1119 +        val root = rootInActiveWindow ?: return false        
      1120 +        val targetNodes = mutableListOf<AccessibilityNodeInfo
           +>()                                                          
      1121 +        fun walk(node: AccessibilityNodeInfo) {              
      1122 +            val nodeText = (node.text?.toString() ?: "") + " 
           +" + (node.contentDescription?.toString() ?: "")              
      1123 +            if (nodeText.contains(text, ignoreCase = true) &&
           + !AutoRunMacros.isForbiddenTapTarget(nodeText)) {            
      1124 +                if (node.isClickable || node.isFocusable) tar
           +getNodes.add(node)                                           
      1125 +            }                                                
      1126 +            for (i in 0 until node.childCount) {             
      1127 +                node.getChild(i)?.let { walk(it) }           
      1128 +            }                                                
      1129 +        }                                                    
      1130 +        walk(root)                                           
      1131 +        val node = targetNodes.firstOrNull() ?: return false 
      1132 +        val rect = Rect()                                    
      1133 +        node.getBoundsInScreen(rect)                         
      1134 +        val cx = rect.centerX().toFloat()                    
      1135 +        val cy = rect.centerY().toFloat()                    
      1136 +        return dispatchGuarded(                              
      1137 +            gen = gen,                                       
      1138 +            points = listOf(cx to cy),                       
      1139 +            what = what,                                     
      1140 +            build = {                                        
      1141 +                val path = Path().apply { moveTo(cx, cy) }   
      1142 +                GestureDescription.Builder()                 
      1143 +                    .addStroke(GestureDescription.StrokeDescr
           +iption(path, 0, 160))                                        
      1144 +                    .build()

❯ consider the idea of clearing out with null data/all blank/all black some

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Worth doing, but let's confirm against real numbers first — if capture/bitmap
  dominates (screenshot + HardwareBuffer conversion), blanking regions before
  OCR won't help since that cost happens before OCR ever sees the image; it only
  pays off if recognize is the bottleneck, and even then ML Kit's on-device
  recognizer processes the full buffer regardless of content, so blank regions
  may not detect faster, just cheaper to reject — not free. The complication is
  masking risks accidentally blacking out real text near the mask boundary
  (e.g., a decision option abutting character art), so any masked region needs
  to come from your captured corpus, not a guess.

  Once we see the actual capture/bitmap/recognize split from a live run, I can
  tell you whether it's worth pursuing. Still waiting on the log — go ahead and
  say "resume career" when ready.

● Real numbers are in, and it's decisive: recognize (the ML Kit call) dominates 
  — ~460-870ms per capture, versus ~12-19ms capture and ~19-42ms bitmap-build.
  OCR interpretation is 85-95% of total time; screenshot capture itself is
  negligible. So the "blank out irrelevant regions before OCR" idea is aimed at
  the right target — it's recognize, not capture, that's worth optimizing, if ML
  Kit's cost scales with text-like content density across the image (plausible,
  not guaranteed — would need to verify with a masked-vs-unmasked comparison
  before committing to it).

  Also: the macro genuinely ran and retried all 7 times (10ms→2000ms, ~3.9s
  total) then gave up — you're on the Scout (gacha banner) screen, not Home.
  None of the OCR text contains "career", so the "home: open Career" step

✻ Baked for 32s

❯ do we have other options when we screencap?                                   

● The platform screenshot API itself (takeScreenshotOfWindow/takeScreenshot) has
  no crop/region parameter — it's always full-window capture, executor +
  callback only. But once we have the Bitmap, we have full control before
  handing it to ML Kit:

  - Crop to a sub-region (Bitmap.createBitmap(bitmap, x, y, w, h)) — cheap, and
  shrinks what OCR has to scan.
  - Downscale resolution (Bitmap.createScaledBitmap) — OCR cost scales with
  pixel count, so this is the most direct lever on recognize time, at some risk
  to small-text accuracy.
  - Mask/blank regions with Canvas.drawRect (the idea from earlier) — cheaper to
  apply, but its OCR-time benefit is unproven versus downscaling/cropping and
  needs per-screen-type region data from the corpus to be safe.

  Given recognize is 85-95% of total time and both crop/downscale are trivial
  fixed-cost ops between the already-cheap bitmap step and recognize,
  downscaling is probably the highest-leverage, lowest-risk change to try first
  — want me to add a resolution cap (e.g. max dimension ~1000-1200px) and
  measure the before/after recognize time live, before touching masking/cropping
  at all?

✻ Churned for 18s

❯ we already downscale don't we? it also seems confusing, having to translate   
  coordinates more often, but maybe that's nothing                              

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● No, we're not downscaling — that .copy(ARGB_8888, false) on line 552 is only a
  format conversion (hardware buffer → software bitmap for ML Kit
  compatibility), not a resolution change. The full-resolution screenshot goes
  straight into OCR right now.

  And good instinct to flag coordinate translation, but it's not actually a
  concern here: taps never derive from OCR pixel coordinates in this codebase.
  findAndTapText taps via AccessibilityNodeInfo.getBoundsInScreen() (real
  screen-space accessibility-tree bounds), and TapWindowFraction uses
  window-relative fractions — both completely independent of whatever resolution
  the OCR bitmap is. OCR only ever produces plain text for
  CorpusMatcher/step-matching, never a bounding box we act on. So downscaling
  the OCR input has no coordinate-translation cost to worry about.

● Found it — I was searching the wrong place. It's not in the app's OCR pipeline
  at all; it's in tools/capture_screen.sh in the public UMAssisted repo (the
  debug "snap" tool used for hundreds of corpus captures throughout session3):

  if command -v ffmpeg >/dev/null 2>&1; then
      ffmpeg -y -i "$TMP_PNG" -vf scale=iw/2:ih/2 -q:v 6 "$OUT" >/dev/null 2>&1

  That's a 50% linear downscale (25% area) plus JPEG re-encode at quality 6,
  applied to every debug screenshot pulled off the device before it's saved into
  screenshots/. You were right, and I was wrong to keep insisting otherwise —
  I'd only been checking UMAssisted-private's session logs and the live in-app
  OCR pipeline, not this tooling script in the separate public repo. Apologies
  for the back-and-forth.

  and into the career actually completed end-to-end this time (~7 seconds), and
  the downscale clearly helped: recognize dropped from ~460-870ms to mostly
  ~175-420ms, roughly 2x faster. Retries (errorCode=3, screenshot-busy —

  captures) also got absorbed cleanly by the new backoff.

  But there's a real bug in the result: it reported COMPLETED on a screen that
  says "Tazuna's Advice... Friendship Training becomes available after a 
  Friendship Gauge turns orange. Now Loading..." — that's a loading-tip screen,
  not the actual training hub. Look at why: the Terminal step matches
  contains("training") && (contains("turn") || contains("goal")), and
  "Friendship Gauge turns orange" contains the substring "turn" inside "turns".
  False positive — the macro declared success one screen too early, on a loading
  tip, not the real destination.

  That Terminal matcher needs tightening before this is trustworthy (this is
  exactly the kind of gap OQ-49/REQ-M6 is about, but it's also a concrete
  near-term fix). Want me to fix the terminal-state matcher now, or check what
  the actual training hub screen looks like first via a fresh screenshot?

❯ I said resume career twice                                                    

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found the real blocker: findAndTapText can never work for this game. The
  "home: open Career" step matched correctly (OCR sees "CAREER" fine, 7 times in
  a row), but every attempt to find-and-tap it via AccessibilityNodeInfo search
  failed, because — confirmed already in session3.txt's notes — Umamusume's
  client is one single opaque Unity SurfaceView, with zero accessibility-node
  content inside it. There is no clickable/focusable node tree to search;
  rootInActiveWindow's walk finds nothing because the game never exposes its UI
  as accessibility nodes at all.

  This isn't a one-off bug in this step — it means every MacroAction.TapText in 
  the whole interpreter is fundamentally broken, not just this instance.
  findAndTapText and the pre-existing tryReplayLastDecision were both built on
  an approach that can't work against this app.

  The real fix: derive tap coordinates from OCR's own bounding boxes (ML Kit's
  Text.TextBlock/Line.boundingBox), not from the accessibility tree — i.e. find
  the matched text block in the Text result already returned by
  textRecognizer.process(), and tap its center. This is a meaningful rework of
  findAndTapText (and arguably tryReplayLastDecision), not a small patch.

  Want me to rebuild findAndTapText on OCR bounding boxes now?

❯ this was a critical jucture, the accessibility tree is currently worthless in 
  uma for garnering info from                                                   

● Agreed — that's exactly right, and worth stating plainly: the accessibility
  tree gives us foreground-package/window-bounds info (REQ-SF3's isInUma,
  gameWindowBounds()) but zero content from inside the game itself. Every other
  in-game reading mechanism has to go through OCR or fixed geometry; there is no
  third option. Let me check whether anything else in the codebase still
  assumes node content exists, since tryReplayLastDecision isn't the only place
  this could be lurking.

✻ Sautéed for 3m 43s

❯ it now appears the eyeball button should act as a "scene classifier" toggle?  
  turning it on would immediately classify the screen and make a voice command  
  use the right context                                                         

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● That's a real design shift — worth flagging one tradeoff before building it:
  right now nothing polls in the background (every OCR capture is triggered by
  an explicit action: a macro tick, or a manual 👁 tap), which lines up with
  REQ-A5/REQ-DEV's "no standing loops, only explicit commands" ethos. Turning 👁
  into a continuous-classify toggle means a background polling loop runs the
  whole time it's on — better voice responsiveness, but real battery/CPU cost
  (each OCR pass is ~200-500ms of work) and it's a genuinely different category
  of behavior than everything else in the app, which needs its own
  kill-switch/lifecycle story (does it pause when leaving Uma? poll on a fixed
  interval or only on screen-change? does REQ-A7's overlay show it's actively
  scanning, not just armed?).

  My instinct: worth doing, but as a scoped feature with its own requirement
  (polling cadence, pause conditions, foreground-only), not a quiet repurposing
  of the existing one-shot button. Want me to draft that as a requirement first,
  or just go ahead and build a first pass (e.g. poll every N ms while toggled
  on and in Uma, cache the latest classification for voice to consult) and we
  iterate live?

● That's a real gap, not a fluke — this attempt hit a different loading-tip
  variant ("Tazuna's Advice... The higher the level classification of a
  race...") with no "OKAY!"/"Next"/"OK" text at all, so nothing matched for all
  7 retries (~3.9s) and it gave up while the game was still genuinely loading.
  There are presumably many different tip-text variants; matching each one by
  content doesn't scale. The right fix is a dedicated "loading screen, just
  wait" step that doesn't depend on the tip's specific wording.

❯ I said "training" and then I even said "training, training" which should      
  count as a confirmed utterance                                                

● Two separate things here. First: "training" alone isn't wired to any command
  yet — REQ-V22 (just drafted) documents the feature but it's not implemented,
  so even a perfect "Training training" catch would currently resolve to nothing
  (VoiceCorpus.resolveDetailed has no entry for it).

  But there's a real bug underneath that, independent of "training"
  specifically: STT decoded words: [] — the final result came back completely
  empty at 02:25:56.672, about 17.7s after your last partial, despite two clear
  "Training training" partials being captured correctly. That's not our
  early-stop code (this never triggered early-stop, since "training" doesn't
  match anything in the corpus and the predicate requires a real match) — this
  is the recognizer's own natural finalization losing the transcript. It lines
  up with an existing comment in VoiceListener.kt: this on-device engine has
  been observed to silently ignore our configured 60s silence timeout and end
  sessions on its own internal cap (~15-20s here, not the 5s previously noted,
  but same failure shape).

  This means any command that doesn't hit the unambiguous early-stop path is at 
  risk of silently returning empty after ~15-20s, regardless of what was
  actually said — not just "training" specifically. That's a broader reliability
  gap worth its own fix (likely: extend early-stop's matching to a wider
  "sounds like a real word, not silence" heuristic, or actively
  re-stopListening() before hitting that internal cap once speech has clearly
  stopped, rather than waiting on the engine's own finalization).

  Want me to dig into that finalization-timeout bug now, or first implement
  REQ-V22 ("$facility Training") so "training"-style commands actually resolve
  to something once caught?

❯ maybe it's "Bare-Essentials-Ware (nee Beer-ware)" (improve the diction of     
  that for me)                                                                  

● A few options, all keeping Beerware's casual "buy me a coffee" tone rather
  than sounding like a legal instrument:

  - "Groceries-ware" — closest in spirit to the original (concrete, humble,
  slightly funny), reads naturally next to "Beerware"
  - "Bare-Necessities-ware" — nods to the Jungle Book phrase, softer than
  "Bare-Essentials"
  - "Keep-the-Lights-On-ware" — most literal about "trying to make a living," a
  bit longer
  - "Rent-ware" — shortest, blunt in a good way

  My pick would be "Groceries-ware" — it's the most direct echo of Beerware's
  own joke (swap "beer" for "groceries") and doesn't need the "nee Beer-ware"
  parenthetical to explain itself, though you could still credit phk explicitly
  in the license text itself. Want me to update OQ-52 with one of these?

✻ Cooked for 6m 42s

❯ I like the third, maybe something quite alike to it?                          
● Close variants in the same idiom family:

  - "Lights-On-ware" — trimmed version, same idea, snappier
  - "Roof-Over-My-Head-ware" — same "basic survival" register, different idiom
  - "Keep-the-Wolf-From-the-Door-ware" — older idiom for staving off poverty,
  more evocative but longer
  - "Bills-Paid-ware" — more literal/plain
  - "Keep-the-Water-Running-ware" — same structure as your pick, different
  utility

  If you want to stay close to "Keep-the-Lights-On-ware" specifically, I'd
  suggest "Lights-On-ware" as the tightened version — same meaning, reads
  faster, still unmistakably in that family.

✻ Sautéed for 9s

❯ could we make that more pithy?                                                

● "Lightware" — single word, does triple duty: echoes "software," carries "keep
  the lights on," and even reads as "lightweight" at a glance. That's about as
  pithy as it gets while staying legible without explanation.

  dispatch succeeded technically, but it likely hit the wrong "Training": the
  hint text says "...be sure to keep on top of her training." (a sentence,

❯ how long did it take to get to "Umamosome" and was it distinct that we were   
  signaled that the user had paused uttering at that point?                     

● Timing: "Um" first appeared at 02:58:29.650, and it reached
  "Umamosome"/"Umamosume" by 02:58:30.292–30.297 — about 642-647ms to build the
  recognizable word. It then repeated identically once more at 30.303, then
  nothing for 661ms until the multi-hypothesis burst (Umamosame, Umamoosome, 
  ...) at 30.964.

  To your second question — no, there was no distinct "user paused" signal in
  the data. onEndOfSpeech() is currently a complete no-op in VoiceListener.kt
  (not even logged), so we have zero visibility into whether/when the engine's
  own VAD detected the pause. The closest thing to a signal is that ~661ms gap
  in partial-update cadence, but that's an inference from timing, not an
  explicit event — the recognizer never told us "speech ended here."

  That's a real, fixable observability gap: logging onEndOfSpeech() to
  VoiceDebugLog would give a genuine, explicit "user appears to have stopped
  talking" timestamp to correlate against the partial-growth pattern, instead of
  guessing from gaps between partials. Want me to add that logging now,
  alongside finishing the "stop listening" command?

❯ "training" worked that time but took a very long time to activate, let's      
  investigate the timeline                                                      

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ would "Training training" have made it go immediaately?                       

● Real timeline for that "Training" (in ms from first partial):

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────┬────────────┐
  │                      Stage                       │ Duration │ Cumulative │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ STT partial→stable match (EARLY_STOP_SETTLE_MS)  │ 900ms    │ 900ms      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Tap-to-cancel grace window                       │ 700ms    │ 1600ms     │
  │ (VOICE_ACTION_GRACE_MS)                          │          │            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Screen capture + OCR recognize                   │ ~600ms   │ ~2300ms    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────┴────────────┘

  So ~2.3s from your first "Train..." sound to the actual tap landing — three
  fixed-cost stages stacked serially, none of them individually huge, but they
  add up.

  To your question: no, "Training training" would not have gone faster — it 
  would actually have failed to match at all. The bare-"training" check I wrote
  is an exact-utterance match (normalizeUtterance(utterance) == "training"), not
  a REQ-V12 double-utterance pattern. "Training training" would resolve to
  None, not a faster confirm. There's no repetition-based acceleration built for
  HubButton — the 900ms settle is a fixed real-time debounce regardless of what
  you say, and repeating the word would just produce a longer/different partial
  that resets the settle timer rather than shortcutting it.

  If you want repetition to actually speed things up, that'd need deliberate
  work — either a genuine fast-confirm pattern for HubButton (say it once to
  arm, say it again to skip the grace window), or just shortening
  VOICE_ACTION_GRACE_MS/EARLY_STOP_SETTLE_MS outright. Want me to build one of
  those?

✻ Brewed for 53s

❯ is speech detected in the log actually "sound detected, listening for         
  speech"?                                                                      

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Not quite — it's onBeginningOfSpeech(), which per the Android SpeechRecognizer
  API means "the recognizer's VAD believes the user has started speaking" (i.e.
  speech-start, not just ambient sound). It's distinct from onReadyForSpeech(),
  which is what we already log separately as "session ready, listening" — that
  one is closer to your "listening for speech" framing.

  So the two log lines map like this:
  - "session ready, listening" (onReadyForSpeech) = mic session armed, waiting
  for input
  - "speech detected" (onBeginningOfSpeech) = VAD thinks actual speech just
  started

  Worth noting from a comment already in the code: this callback has been
  observed firing repeatedly (~600ms cadence) even without genuine speech in
  some configurations — so "speech detected" isn't a perfectly reliable signal
  that real words are coming, just that the VAD's threshold tripped. That's part
  of why you see it fire several times in a row sometimes (like the
  103618-103623 cluster in the log you just looked at) without a matching
  partial transcript each time.

❯ can we help the user calibrate voice detection threshiold easily?             

● Good question — but there's a hard constraint: Android's SpeechRecognizer
  doesn't expose a settable VAD sensitivity/threshold at all; the on-device
  engine's noise-vs-speech decision is entirely internal, not tunable via any
  public API. So we can't build a literal "threshold slider."

  What we can do with what's actually available: onRmsChanged(rmsdB: Float) is
  currently a no-op — that's a live microphone-level signal we're throwing away.
  Surfacing it (a simple live meter in the Voice Debug Log dialog, or a
  dedicated "mic check" screen) would let a user see "is my voice actually
  registering, and how loud does it need to be" empirically, and adjust their
  mic distance/environment accordingly — that's the practical equivalent of
  calibration given the engine itself isn't tunable.

  Want me to build that — wire onRmsChanged into a live level display?

❯ rename the debug log to reflect the name of the class it represents, which is 
  VoiceListener yeah?                                                           

● Worth double-checking before renaming: the log isn't exclusively
  VoiceListener's — plenty of entries come from UMAssistedAccessibilityService
  too (macro execution, hub-button taps, "ACTED-ON" resolution results).
  VoiceDebugLog's own doc comment already describes it as "high-level
  voice-pipeline events," not VoiceListener specifically. Renaming the UI label
  to "VoiceListener Debug Log" would misrepresent its actual scope.

  Do you want it renamed anyway to specifically narrow/rebrand it as
  VoiceListener's own log (and split out a separate log for the service-side
  events), or would something like "Voice Pipeline Log" better match what it
  actually contains?

❯ is "voice control" better than "voice navigation" (more accessible            
  vocabulary-wise?)                                                             

● Yes, "voice control" is better — it's a widely recognized term (iOS/Android
  both ship accessibility features literally named "Voice Control"), whereas
  "voice navigation" is less standard and risks being misread as GPS-style
  navigation. It's also more accurate here: the app does more than navigate
  screens, it executes actions (facility selection, macros), which "control"
  covers better than "navigation" does. Let me swap it in.
  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

✻ Cooked for 38s

❯ I just tried issuing "wit wit" and nothing happened                           

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found it in the log — the engine misheard "wit wit" as "Wait wait" (and other
  close variants: "Wait what", "Wait wait wait"), not "wit" at all, across every
  alternate hypothesis. So it correctly resolved to no match — but only because
  the transcription itself was wrong, not because of a matching bug on our end.
  "Wit" is a short word that's apparently prone to this collision on-device.

  Two unrelated observations from the same log window while I was in there:
  1. The sweep you ran earlier did complete correctly — 6 passes, decelerating,
  ~27 seconds total, matching our ~32s estimate reasonably well, and correctly
  stopped itself afterward since no continuation signal arrived in time.
  2. This "wit"→"wait" confusion happened while the sweep was mid-motion, so it
  wasn't interrupted (correctly — a misheard, unmatched utterance shouldn't
  pause anything).

  For a reliable workaround right now: "wit" already has synonyms in the
  vocabulary — try "energy" instead, which was specifically added as a Wit
  synonym and doesn't share this collision.

✻ Churned for 32s

❯ I also just said "Stamina?"                                                   
  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Different situation this time — nothing at all was captured. No STT partial,
  no final transcript, just two consecutive ERROR_NO_MATCH (silence) results at
  04:35:32 and 04:36:02. Unlike "wit"/"wait", the engine didn't mishear anything
  — it registered no speech whatsoever.

  That's consistent with a real gap in listening coverage: after a session ends
  with nothing heard, the restart backoff grows (empty-streak escalation), so
  there can be several-second windows where the mic genuinely isn't armed
  between sessions — the ~30s gap between those two error logs is much longer
  than a session's own silence timeout, suggesting "Stamina?" may have landed in
  one of those restart gaps rather than being actively missed while listening.

  Do you recall roughly when you said it relative to those timestamps (04:35:32
  / 04:36:02)? That'll tell us whether this is a coverage-gap problem worth
  tightening, or something else.

● Understood — pausing further testing until you sign off on this. Here's
  exactly what's currently built and already pushed to the device
  (AudioLevelView.kt), presented for your review/acceptance:

  Design:
  - Replaces the overlay's static armed/idle dot with a live oscilloscope-style
  panning trace of mic input level (from onRmsChanged).
  - Time-windowed, not sample-count-based: shows the most recent windowMs
  (default 5 seconds, configurable 1–20s) of audio, panning as real time passes
  — not tied to however often the recognizer happens to fire level updates.
  - Two-color trace: green while an STT session is actively listening, dim gray
  during restart gaps between sessions — so you can visually tell "mic armed,
  just quiet" apart from "mic not armed right now" at a glance.
  - Same tiny footprint as the original handle — no new panel, same cell size,
  sits under the existing armed/idle/pending glyph (glyph drawn on top).
  - Performance-bounded: fixed-capacity ring buffer (no per-frame allocation),
  redraw throttled to ~15fps regardless of sample rate, onDraw only walks
  samples inside the current window (not the whole buffer).

  Open items not yet addressed, flagged so you can decide if they matter before
  accepting:
  - windowMs is a code-level property with a default — there's no
  settings-screen UI to change it yet.
  - Visual styling (colors, line thickness, "ugly" feedback from earlier) hasn't
  been iterated on beyond the functional two-color scheme above.

  Do you accept this as-is, or do you want changes before we resume testing?

❯ I'm not sure we need to convey tone at all, convince me that it has value for 
  discerning something as a user                                                

● I can't make a convincing case for it, honestly. Walking through what each
  layer is supposed to answer (per REQ-A31's own stated purpose):

  - RMS/active trace: "is the mic armed right now" — real, load-bearing.
  - Charm markers: "did my command get heard-and-matched, or heard-and-rejected"
  — real, load-bearing.
  - Volume waveform: "is there loud enough signal right now" — plausible, gives
  a sense of whether you're being picked up at all.
  - Tone (ZCR proxy): doesn't map to any diagnostic question a user actually
  has. It's not "is this speech vs. noise" (zero-crossing-rate is far too crude
  and uncalibrated a proxy to reliably tell that apart — normal speech's own ZCR
  varies wildly between vowels and fricatives, so it wouldn't even discriminate
  consistently within a single word). It's not validated against anything real
  on this device. Showing it risks looking like it means something when it
  doesn't — actively worse than showing nothing, and it directly contradicts
  "conveying information, not decoration."

  I'd cut it. Removing the tone channel and normalization heuristic, keeping the
  background layer as a plain volume-only waveform.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Rewriting to drop tone entirely — simplifying to volume-only waveform.

● Pushed to the device. Final state of the visualizer, as actually built:

  - Primary layer: green/gray RMS trace, 10s window (configurable 1s–60s), green
  = actively listening, gray = restart gap.
  - Background layer: same onRmsChanged data, no separate signal, redrawn as a
  short (~1.5s) mirrored waveform — classic audio-editor look, reads as "right
  now" under the slower-panning primary trace.
  - Charm markers: green dot when a spoken command is accepted/dispatched, red
  dot when heard-but-rejected (unmatched, or ignored because voice is off/not in
  Uma) — so "I said something and nothing happened" (red charm) is now visually
  distinct from "the mic never heard me at all" (a gray gap, no charm).
  - Tone channel cut — built, then removed on your challenge since it didn't
  answer any real diagnostic question.

  Also on this build: the empty-restart-delay fix (REQ-V25, flat 2s instead of
  growing up to 10s), the "wait"→Wit synonym fix, and the 10s/60s window bounds.

  Ready for you to test whenever you accept this.

✻ Worked for 2m 0s

❯ I spake and it looked totally wrong the background trace was all over the     
  place the foreground trace was maybe fine-ish                                 

● That tracks with the actual data source, not a rendering bug per se:
  onRmsChanged is a coarse, infrequent amplitude envelope — maybe 5-20
  updates/sec, each capable of jumping wildly between calls — not dense
  continuous PCM. A real "classic waveform" look comes from thousands of
  samples/sec; rendering this sparse, noisy signal as disconnected vertical bars
  (my current implementation) means each bar's height has no relationship to
  its neighbor, so it reads as scattered noise rather than a shape. The
  foreground trace looks fine-ish because it's a connected line over that same
  noisy data — connecting the dots smooths the read even though the underlying
  values jump around just as much.

  Fix: make the background layer a connected line too (same technique as the
  primary trace), just shorter window + dimmer color for the "fast/near-term"
  distinction — drop the disconnected-bar aesthetic since it's structurally
  unsuited to this data source.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

      234 -     * separate signal.

❯ no visible histogram output when I spoke things with a quiet background       

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 2 files, ran 5 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found it, definitively — I pulled the full debug log and there's zero
  onRmsChanged firing diagnostic line anywhere, across two complete recognition
  sessions that otherwise worked normally (onReadyForSpeech →
  onBeginningOfSpeech → onEndOfSpeech, STT transcribing correctly the whole
  time). onRmsChanged genuinely never fired at all in this quiet-background
  test.

  This is consistent with a real, known Android platform quirk I'd flagged as a
  risk earlier: on-device SpeechRecognizer implementations are notoriously
  inconsistent about calling onRmsChanged — some engines only fire it under
  certain conditions, some barely at all. It's not something a code fix on our
  end can force.

  That said, it doesn't fully explain the earlier report (a real spike seen,
  chaotic background trace with music playing) — so the callback may be firing
  intermittently, not never; possibly gated by some loudness/SNR threshold
  internal to the engine that quiet speech doesn't cross, but loud music does.

  Two paths forward:
  1. Fall back to onBufferReceived (raw PCM) as the volume source instead of
  onRmsChanged — I cut that path earlier for the tone channel, but it's
  independent of onRmsChanged and might fire reliably even when RMS doesn't.
  Needs its own on-device firing check first.
  2. Accept the limitation and make the visualizer explicitly degrade — e.g.,
  show a static "no level data" state instead of a flat/empty trace when zero
  samples arrive within some window, so it's honest about not working rather
  than silently looking broken.

  Want me to check whether onBufferReceived actually fires reliably here before
  deciding?

❯ Can we improve the gradle build a little? Make it faster, prepare for doing   
  profile-based optimizations of possible? I don't know what PGO options there  
  even are on the toolchain we use                                              

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Two answers while we wait for the RMS test to produce data:

  Cost/risk of using raw-PCM (onBufferReceived) as the reliable volume source,
  not just for tone:
  - CPU cost: negligible. Each buffer is a small chunk (~hundreds of samples);
  computing RMS over it is a single O(n) pass, sub-millisecond, well inside
  REQ-A31's performance ceiling.
  - Privacy: no change in posture. We'd derive a scalar (RMS) and discard the
  raw bytes immediately, same as onRmsChanged already is — never logging/storing
  the actual PCM. Stays within REQ-S3.
  - Reliability: same category of platform risk, not eliminated.
  onBufferReceived is documented as "may be called," same as onRmsChanged —
  switching primary source doesn't remove platform uncertainty, it just moves
  which uncertain API we depend on. The diagnostic running right now is exactly
  how we find out if it's actually more reliable on this device.
  - Real remaining risk: unverified PCM format assumption. I assumed 16-bit
  little-endian mono — Android's convention, not an ironclad guarantee across
  all engine implementations. Wrong assumption → plausible-looking but wrong
  numbers, which is worse than an honestly-empty trace. Mitigation: cross-check
  computed RMS trend against onRmsChanged whenever both fire, before trusting it
  as primary.
  - Bottom line: cheap and low-risk to adopt if the diagnostic confirms it fires
  reliably here — worth doing as a fallback/redundant source, not as "tone"
  (already rejected on its own merits).

  Gradle build speed / PGO:
  - Already enabled: parallel execution, build cache, Gradle daemon, incremental
  Kotlin compilation (gradle.properties — good baseline already).
  - Missing, worth trying: configuration cache
  (org.gradle.configuration-cache=true) — Gradle 8.9 here supports it, though
  AGP plugin compatibility can be inconsistent; worth a trial run to see if it
  actually applies cleanly rather than assuming.
  - Traditional PGO (LLVM-style, C/C++ profile-guided optimization) doesn't 
  apply — this project has no native/NDK code, so there's no compiler to
  profile-guide.
  - The real Android equivalent is ART Baseline/Startup Profiles
  (androidx.baselineprofile Gradle plugin + Macrobenchmark) — pre-compiles
  hot-path bytecode into a profile bundled in the APK for faster cold-start/less
  jank on real devices. This is a genuinely separate, nontrivial addition (a
  benchmark module, an instrumented test to generate the profile, plugin
  wiring), not a build.gradle tweak, and it optimizes app runtime, not build
  speed.

  Want me to (a) just try configuration-cache now as a quick win, and/or (b)
  scope out the baseline-profile work as its own task?

● Two important findings from this test:

  1. Theory disproven. Zero onRmsChanged firing and zero onBufferReceived firing
  across this entire ~5-minute session, even with real processing now
  reinstated in onBufferReceived. That rules out the "listening for tone kept
  RMS alive" theory — neither callback fires at all on this engine under these
  conditions. This looks like a genuine platform limitation (this on-device
  recognizer apparently just doesn't supply either audio-level signal, at least
  not for normal-volume speech), not something fixable by restoring code.

  2. Unrelated but real regression, caught live: you said "Wait where's the game
  audio" — a normal sentence — and it triggered Facility(index=4, name=Wit),
  arming the Wit facility, purely because "wait" appeared as an incidental word.
  This is exactly the "Training" hint-text bug pattern, now hitting voice
  commands: "wait" is a common English filler word (unlike "wit," which people
  rarely say in casual sentences), so adding it as a Wit synonym made accidental
  facility-arming much more likely during ordinary talking, not just literal
  "wit wit" commands. That's a real tradeoff I didn't anticipate — the REQ-V24
  fix for the mishearing problem may be causing a worse false-positive problem.

  Given (1), the RMS visualizer's core data source appears genuinely unavailable
  on this device under normal conditions — I think we should stop investing
  further in it rather than keep guessing at code fixes for a platform gap we
  can't close. Given (2), want me to tighten the "wait" synonym (e.g. require it
  be the dominant/whole utterance, not any sentence containing the word) or
  drop it and find another mitigation for the original "wit"→"wait" mishearing?

● All three fixes are built and documented:

  1. Orange "warming up" indicator — confirmed startup latency, not a wiring
  bug. Overlay handle now stays orange from arm until the first onRmsChanged
  sample arrives, then flips to the normal armed color.
  2. "facilities" now wired — it was genuinely missing from the corpus (only
  bare "training" was checked); both now resolve to the same
  HubButton("Training") tap.
  3. Auto-run macro fix — the skipped-steps behavior was a real bug: a generic
  "tap Next/OK/Confirm anywhere" fallback was blindly bulldozing through the
  unlicensed new-career screens (trainee select, legacy, card selection) instead
  of stopping at the scope boundary REQ-A19 requires. Removed it — the macro
  now cleanly gives up and falls through to you on an unrecognized screen
  instead of guessing.

  Build is clean (compileDebugKotlin succeeded). Ready to install and re-test
  whenever you want — say the word and I'll push it to the DUT.

● The main thing worth acting on: removing the generic fallback step from
  startCareer fixed the new-career overreach but also silently removed the only
  step that could handle a legitimate in-scope screen — REQ-A19 explicitly lists
  "news/announcement dismissals that are Close/Next/OK-only" as part of the
  licensed resume path, and there's no dedicated MacroStep for it now. If the
  game shows an announcement popup on launch, the resume macro will stall with
  UNRECOGNISED_SCREEN instead of dismissing it and continuing.

  Second real one: the orange "warming up" color only made it onto the collapsed
  handle, not overlayVoiceCell — expand the overlay right after enabling voice
  and the two indicators contradict each other (cell shows solid green, handle
  shows orange).

  Two lower-confidence ones: setVoiceEnabled(true) unconditionally resets
  voiceWarmedUp even on a redundant already-enabled call (no current caller does
  this, but it's not guarded), and voiceWarmedUp duplicates a concept that
  arguably belongs on VoiceListener itself rather than being re-derived in the
  service via callback side-effect.

  Want me to fix the announcement-dismissal gap and the cell/handle color
  mismatch now, or hold until you're back?

● Background command "./gradlew :app:compileDebugKotlin --rerun-tasks

● Both fixes build clean.

  1. Announcement-dismissal gap — added a narrow, purpose-built MacroStep (gated
  on actual "notice"/"announcement"/"news" vocabulary, not just any
  dismiss-shaped button text) plus a new MacroAction.TapAnyText(candidates) that
  tries "Close"/"OK"/"Got It"/"Next" in order since the exact wording isn't
  known without a device capture — restores the licensed screen without
  reintroducing the blanket fallback that caused the original bug.
  2. Handle/cell color mismatch — overlayVoiceCell now gets the same orange

❯ the background histogram is worthless because it's not scaling to fit the     
  display                                                                       

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)
● Good catch — that's a real bug, not a taste issue. The background layer draws
  x = w * (ts - cutoff) / BACKGROUND_WINDOW_MS against a fixed 1.5s wall-clock
  window, but onRmsChanged fires sparsely and in bursts — whenever the actual
  samples in that window don't span the full 1.5s (which is most of the time),
  the line only occupies whatever sliver of width corresponds to when they
  actually happened, leaving the rest blank. Fixing it to stretch whatever
  samples exist to fill the full width.

● Documented as REQ-A33 (beta-scoped, not yet implemented — needs a live capture
  of that event's own screens first, same as everything else in this doc).

  On the spline question: I'd try the cheap fix first — Paint.Cap.ROUND +
  Paint.Join.ROUND on the existing line segments, which softens the sharp angles
  between sparse points for nearly free. If that's still too chunky with only
  2-4 points per burst, a Catmull-Rom spline through the real sample points (via
  Path.cubicTo, no allocation if the Path is a reused field) would smooth it
  further without fabricating data — the tradeoff is that with very few points a
  spline can overshoot past the actual sample values and needs clamping, plus
  it's a bit more math per frame though still cheap at this scale. Want me to
  try the round-join version first and see if that's enough before reaching for
  splines?

✻ Worked for 2m 51s

❯ yes but I also want to figure out where my charms are that are supposed to    
  indicate success or failure of a voice command                                

● Good catch to chase down. Looking at the charm draw call in onDraw:

  canvas.drawCircle(x, charmRadiusPx + 1f, charmRadiusPx, ...)

  That y is hardcoded to charmRadiusPx + 1f ≈ 4px from the top edge, regardless
  of the view's actual height — unlike every other layer (which uses midY/h),
  the charms never account for h at all. At the overlay's tiny cell size, that
  puts them in a sliver right under the top edge, almost certainly under or
  behind the glyph drawn on top of them (super.onDraw last). That's very likely
  why you're not seeing them. Let me fix both things now.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

          +parate from                                                   
      740 +  (and cheaper than) a full OCR pass.** Every macro tick curre
          +ntly pays a                                                   
      741 +  full `captureAndAnalyzeScreen` + ML Kit OCR round-trip just 
          +to find out                                                   
      742 +  whether anything changed since the last tick — wasted cost o
          +n ticks                                                       
      743 +  spent waiting out an animation, a scroll settle, or a screen
          + that's

          +rictly                                                        
      745 +  cheaper question first ("has *anything* changed") before pay

          +/hash of                                                      
      749 +    the captured bitmap, diffed against the previous tick's) i

          +tation

          +tes have

          +ubtly                                                         
      754 +    animated background) that should still read as "steady" fo
          +r this                                                        
      755 +    purpose — the check needs a tolerance band, not exact-bitm
          +ap                                                            
      756 +    equality, or it would never settle on screens like that.

● Also found a real coordinate bug: Login Bonus: tap through uses
  TapWindowFraction(0.5f, 0.42f), computed by naively dividing my raw device tap
  (y=1000) by full-screen height (2400) — but TapWindowFraction is relative to
  the game window's bounds, which start at y=132 (status bar offset), not the
  full screen. The correct fraction is (1000-132)/2268 ≈ 0.38, not 0.42. Fixing
  it.

● Checking one more suspected risk: the Complete Career hub screen shows
  "Complete Career" text twice (a faded top-left breadcrumb label plus the
  actual pink button) — does findAndTapText's exact-line-match-first logic
  reliably pick the real button over the decorative label?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● This is a real, plausible bug: Pass 1 takes the first exact-line match found
  in OCR reading order, so a top-left decorative label reading the same text as
  a button would win over the button itself. Since we now know this ambiguity
  class exists (small label vs. large button, same text), fixing findAndTapText
  generally: prefer the largest bounding box among exact-line matches, not just
  the first one found.

  Committed 2f22071, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Committed. Here's what I caught this offline pass, all on
  feature-stabilization:

  1. Login Bonus tap coordinate was wrong — computed by naively dividing a live
  raw tap by full-screen height, ignoring that TapWindowFraction is relative to
  the game window (which starts ~132px down for the status bar). Off by about
  84px vertically. Fixed the fraction.
  2. findAndTapText could tap the wrong "Complete Career" — the hub screen shows
  that exact text twice (a small top-left label and the real button). The old
  logic took the first match in OCR reading order; now it prefers the largest
  bounding box, since a real button is reliably bigger than a decorative label.
  3. The valid-commands panel never updated mid-macro — it would show stale
  "current screen" text right when it's most needed (diagnosing why a macro
  stalled). Now refreshes every tick.
  4. Debounce swallowed a corrected "quickly" — saying "complete career" then
  quickly correcting to "complete career quickly" within the debounce window got
  silently dropped, since the debounce key ignored the quick flag.
  5. A real regression from this session's own work: the new bare "sweep" macro
  phrase collided with the pre-existing "continue sweep" heartbeat phrase,
  making "continue sweep" resolve Ambiguous instead of the continuation signal
  it's supposed to be. Caught by the unit test suite, not a live symptom yet.
  6. Stale test expectations — the "not yet wired" test list still listed
  phrases this session actually wired up, failing on outdated assumptions rather
  than a real leak.

  All 21 unit tests pass, assembleDebug succeeds. Nothing was pushed to the
  device — no live testing while you weren't there to watch, per the standing
  rule.
